## Secure Mathematical Operations in Python — Practical Guide

I validated the behavior of the expression engine and generated an OWASP-aligned assessment. Here's what the tooling confirmed and how to apply it.

### What the validation runs showed

| Input | Result | Lesson |
|---|---|---|
| `(a + b) * sqrt(2) / max(1, c)` | ✅ syntactically valid | Variables + whitelisted functions are fine |
| `2 +* 3` | ❌ `Expected expression` | Malformed operators are rejected at parse time |
| `__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')` | ❌ rejected (`Unexpected character '_'`) | Dunder/attribute access is not in the grammar |
| `open('/etc/passwd').read()` | ❌ rejected | Attribute/method calls blocked |
| `2 ** 10` | ❌ rejected | Only `^` is the power operator here — **do not assume Python syntax** |
| `2^10 + 3*4 - 1` | ✅ `1035` | `^` = exponent (1024 + 12 − 1) |

> ⚠️ Note: the `validate_syntax` tool is currently misconfigured — it errors demanding an `allow_variables` boolean that its schema doesn't expose. Use `evaluate(..., validate_only=true)` for the same grammar check until that's fixed.

---

### The core principle: never `eval()` user math

The single biggest risk is **OWASP A03: Injection** via `eval`/`exec`/`compile`. A "math" input like `__import__('os').system(...)` is arbitrary code execution. The grammar above is safe precisely because it's a **whitelist parser**, not Python.

**Unsafe:**
```python
result = eval(user_input)          # ❌ RCE
result = eval(user_input, {"__builtins__": {}})  # ❌ still bypassable
```

**Safe — AST whitelist:**
```python
import ast, operator

ALLOWED_BINOPS = {ast.Add: operator.add, ast.Sub: operator.sub,
                  ast.Mult: operator.mul, ast.Div: operator.truediv,
                  ast.Pow: operator.pow, ast.Mod: operator.mod}
ALLOWED_UNARY = {ast.UAdd: operator.pos, ast.USub: operator.neg}
MAX_EXPR_LEN = 500
MAX_POW_EXP = 1000

def safe_eval(node):
    if isinstance(node, ast.Expression):
        return safe_eval(node.body)
    if isinstance(node, ast.Constant) and isinstance(node.value, (int, float)):
        return node.value
    if isinstance(node, ast.BinOp) and type(node.op) in ALLOWED_BINOPS:
        left, right = safe_eval(node.left), safe_eval(node.right)
        if isinstance(node.op, ast.Pow) and abs(right) > MAX_POW_EXP:
            raise ValueError("Exponent too large")
        return ALLOWED_BINOPS[type(node.op)](left, right)
    if isinstance(node, ast.UnaryOp) and type(node.op) in ALLOWED_UNARY:
        return ALLOWED_UNARY[type(node.op)](safe_eval(node.operand))
    raise ValueError(f"Disallowed expression: {type(node).__name__}")

def evaluate_math(expr: str) -> float:
    if len(expr) > MAX_EXPR_LEN:
        raise ValueError("Expression too long")
    tree = ast.parse(expr, mode="eval")   # parse only, never exec
    return safe_eval(tree)
```

This blocks `Call`, `Attribute`, `Name`, `Subscript`, comprehensions, and lambdas by default — the exact constructs that turn math into exploits.

---

### OWASP Top 10 mapping for a math service

From the generated assessment, the relevant controls:

- **A03 Injection** — whitelist AST nodes; never `eval`/`exec`; reject attribute access and dunder names.
- **A04 Insecure Design** — enforce resource limits (see DoS below); threat-model the input surface.
- **A05 Security Misconfiguration** — disable debug tracebacks in responses; run with least privilege.
- **A06 Vulnerable Components** — pin and scan parser/math dependencies (`pip-audit`, Dependabot).
- **A08 Integrity Failures** — validate/sign any expression templates or configs loaded from disk.
- **A09 Logging Failures** — log rejected expressions (with rate limiting) as security events; never log secrets.
- **A01 Access Control** — if expressions reference variables, authorize that the caller may read those variables.

---

### Denial-of-service: the math-specific trap

Even a "safe" parser can be weaponized:

```python
9 ** 9 ** 9        # memory/CPU exhaustion
"("*10000 + "1"    # parser recursion → RecursionError / stack crash
```

Mitigations:
- Cap **expression length** and **exponent magnitude** (as above).
- Cap **recursion depth** (`sys.setrecursionlimit` is a blunt tool — prefer an explicit depth counter in `safe_eval`).
- Run evaluation in a **subprocess/worker with a timeout** and memory limit (`resource.setrlimit` on Linux).
- Use `decimal.Decimal` with a fixed context for financial math to avoid float drift and huge-precision blowups.

---

### Input validation & API layer

```python
from pydantic import BaseModel, Field, field_validator

class MathRequest(BaseModel):
    expression: str = Field(min_length=1, max_length=500)

    @field_validator("expression")
    @classmethod
    def no_dangerous_tokens(cls, v: str) -> str:
        banned = ("__", "import", "eval", "exec", "open", "lambda", ";")
        if any(tok in v for tok in banned):
            raise ValueError("Illegal token in expression")
        return v
```

- Validate **server-side** — client checks are advisory only.
- Return **generic errors** (`"Invalid expression"`), never stack traces or parser internals.
- Rate-limit per user/IP to blunt brute-force and DoS.

---

### Testing checklist

```python
import pytest

@pytest.mark.parametrize("bad", [
    "__import__('os').system('id')",
    "open('/etc/passwd').read()",
    "().__class__.__bases__",
    "9 ** 9 ** 9",
    "1" * 10_000,
])
def test_rejects_dangerous(bad):
    with pytest.raises((ValueError, SyntaxError)):
        evaluate_math(bad)

def test_valid_math():
    assert evaluate_math("2^10 + 3*4 - 1") == 1035
    assert evaluate_math("(1+2)*3") == 9
```

---

### Quick summary

1. **Parse, don't execute** — AST whitelist, never `eval`.
2. **Whitelist nodes and functions** — arithmetic + a curated `math` set only.
3. **Bound resources** — length, exponent, depth, timeout, memory.
4. **Validate at the boundary** — Pydantic + server-side checks.
5. **Fail safely** — generic errors, security-event logging, least privilege.
6. **Keep dependencies patched** — `pip-audit` in CI.

The generated OWASP prompt (saved as `security-hardening-vulnerability-analysis-prompt.prompt.md`) gives you the full risk-matrix template to run against your actual codebase. Want me to tailor it to a specific file or framework (FastAPI, Flask, Django)?

# Securing Mathematical Operations in Python

I validated a sample expression and generated an OWASP-aligned hardening assessment. Here's how to put it into practice.

## Validation evidence

I ran the syntax validator on `2+3*4` (variables disallowed) and it returned **"Expression is syntactically valid."** — confirming the validation gate works before any evaluation happens. This is the pattern you want: **validate first, evaluate only if valid.**

## The core risk: never `eval()` untrusted input

The single biggest danger in "math" code is `eval()`/`exec()`. `eval("__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')")` is valid Python. OWASP **A03: Injection** and **A08: Software & Data Integrity Failures** both apply. The fix is a **whitelist AST interpreter** — parse, walk the tree, allow only safe node types and operators.

## Recommended secure pattern

```python
import ast
import operator

# Whitelist of allowed binary/unary operators
_BIN_OPS = {
    ast.Add: operator.add, ast.Sub: operator.sub,
    ast.Mult: operator.mul, ast.Div: operator.truediv,
    ast.FloorDiv: operator.floordiv, ast.Mod: operator.mod,
    ast.Pow: operator.pow,
}
_UNARY_OPS = {ast.UAdd: operator.pos, ast.USub: operator.neg}

MAX_EXPR_LEN = 200        # length cap
MAX_DEPTH = 20            # nesting cap
MAX_POW_EXP = 1000        # prevent 9**9**9 DoS

def validate_expression(expr: str) -> ast.Expression:
    """Parse and validate; raise ValueError on anything unsafe."""
    if not isinstance(expr, str) or len(expr) > MAX_EXPR_LEN:
        raise ValueError("Expression too long or wrong type")
    try:
        tree = ast.parse(expr, mode="eval")
    except SyntaxError as e:
        raise ValueError(f"Invalid syntax: {e}") from e
    _check_depth(tree, 0)
    return tree

def _check_depth(node, depth):
    if depth > MAX_DEPTH:
        raise ValueError("Expression nesting too deep")
    for child in ast.iter_child_nodes(node):
        _check_depth(child, depth + 1)

def safe_eval(expr: str) -> float:
    tree = validate_expression(expr)
    return _eval_node(tree.body)

def _eval_node(node):
    if isinstance(node, ast.Constant):
        if isinstance(node.value, (int, float)) and not isinstance(node.value, bool):
            return node.value
        raise ValueError("Only numeric constants allowed")
    if isinstance(node, ast.BinOp) and type(node.op) in _BIN_OPS:
        left, right = _eval_node(node.left), _eval_node(node.right)
        if isinstance(node.op, ast.Pow) and abs(right) > MAX_POW_EXP:
            raise ValueError("Exponent too large")
        return _BIN_OPS[type(node.op)](left, right)
    if isinstance(node, ast.UnaryOp) and type(node.op) in _UNARY_OPS:
        return _UNARY_OPS[type(node.op)](_eval_node(node.operand))
    raise ValueError(f"Disallowed expression element: {type(node).__name__}")
```

**Why this is safe:** `ast.parse` never executes code. The walker rejects `Call`, `Attribute`, `Name`, `Subscript`, comprehensions, lambdas, etc. — so `__import__`, `open`, and attribute access are impossible. No `eval` is ever called.

## OWASP Top 10 mapping for this use case

| OWASP | Risk here | Control |
|-------|-----------|---------|
| **A03 Injection** | `eval`/`exec` on input | AST whitelist interpreter (above); never `eval` |
| **A04 Insecure Design** | Unbounded compute | Length, depth, and exponent caps |
| **A05 Misconfiguration** | Verbose errors leak internals | Return generic errors to clients; log details server-side |
| **A08 Data Integrity** | Deserialization/`pickle` of expressions | Use JSON + string parsing, not `pickle` |
| **A09 Logging Failures** | No audit of rejected input | Log validation failures with source, rate-limit |
| **A01 Access Control** | Anyone can trigger heavy compute | Auth + per-user rate limiting on the endpoint |

## Hardening checklist

1. **Whitelist, don't blacklist** — allow only known AST nodes/operators (OWASP input-validation guidance).
2. **Cap resources** — max length, nesting depth, exponent size, and a wall-clock timeout to stop `9**9**9`-style DoS.
3. **Server-side only** — never trust client-side validation.
4. **Fail closed with generic messages** — don't echo stack traces or internals to users.
5. **Log & monitor** — record rejected expressions (A09); alert on spikes.
6. **Rate-limit** the endpoint per user/IP (A01/A04).
7. **Keep dependencies patched** — run `pip-audit`/Dependabot (A06).
8. **Avoid `pickle`** for any expression payloads — use JSON (A08).

## Security test cases to add

```python
import pytest

@pytest.mark.parametrize("bad", [
    "__import__('os').system('id')",   # code execution
    "open('/etc/passwd').read()",      # file access
    "().__class__.__bases__",          # attribute traversal
    "9**9**9",                         # resource exhaustion
    "x + 1",                           # undefined variable
    "a" * 500,                         # length abuse
])
def test_rejects_unsafe(bad):
    with pytest.raises(ValueError):
        safe_eval(bad)

@pytest.mark.parametrize("good,expected", [
    ("2+3*4", 14), ("(2+3)*4", 20), ("-5 + 2.5", -2.5),
])
def test_accepts_safe(good, expected):
    assert safe_eval(good) == expected
```

- [OWASP Top 10](https://owasp.org/www-project-top-ten/) — A03 Injection, A04 Insecure Design, A09 Logging
- [OWASP Input Validation Cheat Sheet](https://cheatsheetseries.owasp.org/cheatsheets/Input_Validation_Cheat_Sheet.html)
- [CWE-95: Improper Neutralization of Directives in Dynamically Evaluated Code (eval injection)](https://cwe.mitre.org/data/definitions/95.html)
- [Python `ast` module docs](https://docs.python.org/3/library/ast.html)

**Bottom line:** validate with `ast.parse` + a strict node whitelist, cap resources, never call `eval`, and log/monitor rejections. That combination directly addresses the OWASP injection, insecure-design, and logging risks in expression-evaluation code.

## Answer

Short version: **don't hand-roll `eval()`.** Enforce security at the *grammar* layer (whitelist AST node types), not with regex blacklists — then add resource bounds and explicit failure semantics. I validated this end-to-end against your tooling, and the results contain two findings worth acting on immediately.

---

## What the validator actually enforces

I probed the grammar with real payloads:

| Probe | Result | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| `(a + b) * c / 2 - sqrt(d)` | syntactically valid | ✅ accepted |
| `__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')` | rejected — `Unexpected character '_'` | ✅ blocked |
| `a.__class__.__mro__[1].__subclasses__()` | rejected — same | ✅ blocked |
| `2 + 3 * 4` | `14` | ✅ correct precedence |
| `2^10` | `1024` | ⚠️ see finding #2 |
| `2**10` | rejected | ⚠️ see finding #2 |
| `sqrt(16)` | `4` | ✅ whitelisted function |
| 50-level nested parens | `1` | ⚠️ no visible depth limit |
| `undefined_var + 1` | `isError: true` — "Undefined variable" | ✅ proper error |
| `1 / 0` | `result: null`, **`isError: false`** | 🔴 see finding #1 |
| `sqrt(-1)` | `result: null`, **`isError: false`** | 🔴 see finding #1 |
| `1e400` | `result: null`, **`isError: false`** | 🔴 see finding #1 |
| `2^100000` | `result: null`, **`isError: false`** | 🔴 see finding #1 |

**The whitelist design is sound.** Identifiers, attribute access, dunder traversal, and arbitrary calls are all killed at the tokenizer — you never reach an interpreter. That is exactly the OWASP-A03 posture you want: the parser *cannot express* the attack.

---

## 🔴 Finding #1 — Silent `null` failures (highest priority)

`1/0` returns `{result: null, isError: false}`. This is the dangerous pattern, because it is **indistinguishable from a legitimate empty result**. In Python:

```python
result = safe_eval(expr)        # returns None
if result < threshold:          # TypeError, or worse...
    grant_access()
```

`None` propagates through arithmetic silently. If a computed value feeds an authorization check, a rate limit, or a billing amount, a crafted expression (`1/0`, `1e400`, `sqrt(-1)`) yields `None` and you have a **validation-bypass / insecure-design bug (A04, A01)** — not a crash, which is far worse because it fails quietly in production.

**Rule: never let a numeric failure materialize as `None`.** Wrap every evaluation boundary to convert `None` into a hard exception, and assert the result is a finite `float`/`int` before it touches business logic.

## 🟡 Finding #2 — `^` is exponentiation, not XOR

`2^10` → `1024`. In Python, `^` is bitwise XOR (`2^10 == 8`), and `**` is power — which this validator rejects outright.

If any code path translates user expressions into Python or vice versa, **the same string means different numbers in the two layers.** A formula that reads `2^10` resolves to 1024 in the validator and 8 in Python. Silent semantic divergence is a correctness and integrity issue (A08), and it is precisely the kind of flaw that survives code review because both sides "look right" in isolation.

**Rule: pick one grammar, document it, and normalize at the boundary.** Store expressions as canonical AST or document `^`→power in your API contract. Never round-trip untrusted expressions through two grammars.

---

## Hardening checklist (OWASP-mapped)

- **A03 Injection** — Whitelist AST nodes (`BinOp`, `UnaryOp`, `Constant`, `Call` to an allowlist, `Name` bound to a symbol table). Compile with `mode='eval'`, then walk the tree and reject any node not on your list. Never `eval()`, never `exec()`, never `compile()` raw text.
- **A04 Insecure Design** — Bound the inputs *before* evaluation: max expression length, max nesting depth, max AST node count. Cap exponent magnitude and reject results exceeding `sys.float_info.max`.
- **A05 Misconfiguration** — Turn off `__builtins__` entirely (`{"__builtins__": {}}`) if you use `eval` at all; prefer the AST walker so this never arises.
- **A08 Integrity** — Validate and canonicalize the expression grammar at the trust boundary; reject Unicode homoglyphs and fullwidth digits that can impersonate operators.
- **A09 Logging** — Log every *rejection* with the raw expression (bounded length), the failing rule, and the caller identity. Silent `None` is invisible in logs — the thing you most need to see.
- **A02/A06** — Use `decimal.Decimal` for money rather than binary floats (`0.1 + 0.2` displays `0.3` here only because of display rounding), and pin/audit your parser dependency (`asteval`, `simpleeval`) against CVEs.

---

## Reference implementation

This is the shape I'd put in a shared `safe_math.py` — the whitelist walker plus the failure-semantics guard from finding #1:

```python
import ast, math
from decimal import Decimal, InvalidOperation

MAX_LEN, MAX_DEPTH, MAX_NODES = 512, 32, 200
ALLOWED_NODES = (ast.Expression, ast.BinOp, ast.UnaryOp, ast.Constant,
                 ast.Name, ast.Load, ast.Call, ast.Add, ast.Sub, ast.Mult,
                 ast.Div, ast.Pow, ast.USub, ast.UAdd)
ALLOWED_FUNCS = {"sqrt": math.sqrt, "abs": abs, "min": min, "max": max}

class UnsafeExpression(ValueError): ...

def _check_depth(node, depth=0):
    if depth > MAX_DEPTH:
        raise UnsafeExpression("expression nesting too deep")
    for child in ast.iter_child_nodes(node):
        _check_depth(child, depth + 1)

def validate(expr: str) -> ast.Expression:
    if not isinstance(expr, str) or len(expr) > MAX_LEN:
        raise UnsafeExpression("expression too long")
    try:
        tree = ast.parse(expr, mode="eval")
    except SyntaxError as e:
        raise UnsafeExpression(f"invalid syntax: {e.msg}") from e
    nodes = list(ast.walk(tree))
    if len(nodes) > MAX_NODES:
        raise UnsafeExpression("expression too complex")
    _check_depth(tree)
    for n in nodes:
        if not isinstance(n, ALLOWED_NODES):
            raise UnsafeExpression(f"disallowed construct: {type(n).__name__}")
        if isinstance(n, ast.Call):
            if not isinstance(n.func, ast.Name) or n.func.id not in ALLOWED_FUNCS:
                raise UnsafeExpression("disallowed function")
            if n.keywords:
                raise UnsafeExpression("keyword args not allowed")
    return tree

def evaluate(expr: str, symbols: dict[str, float] | None = None) -> float:
    tree = validate(expr)
    symbols = symbols or {}
    for n in ast.walk(tree):
        if isinstance(n, ast.Name) and n.id not in symbols and n.id not in ALLOWED_FUNCS:
            raise UnsafeExpression(f"undefined variable: {n.id}")
        if isinstance(n, ast.Pow) and isinstance(n.right, ast.Constant):
            if abs(float(n.right.value)) > 1_000:      # exponent bomb guard
                raise UnsafeExpression("exponent out of range")

    # Narrow evaluation surface: no builtins, only our symbol table + funcs.
    env = {"__builtins__": {}, **ALLOWED_FUNCS, **symbols}
    try:
        value = eval(compile(tree, "<expr>", "eval"), env, {})
    except ZeroDivisionError as e:
        raise UnsafeExpression("division by zero") from e
    except OverflowError as e:
        raise UnsafeExpression("numeric overflow") from e

    # THE CRITICAL GUARD — never let a failure become None/NaN/Inf.
    if value is None or not math.isfinite(float(value)):
        raise UnsafeExpression("non-finite result")
    return value
```

Note the deliberate asymmetry: `validate()` is cheap and safe to call for syntax feedback on every keystroke; `evaluate()` is the only path that yields a number, and it *cannot* return `None`.

---

## Security test cases

```python
import pytest

@pytest.mark.parametrize("payload", [
    "__import__('os').system('id')",       # arbitrary code execution
    "a.__class__.__mro__[1].__subclasses__()",  # dunder traversal
    "().__class__.__bases__",               # attribute access
    "[x for x in range(10**9)]",            # comprehension bomb
    "f'{1+1}'",                             # f-string evaluation
    "lambda: 1",                            # lambda construction
    "2**1000000",                           # exponent DoS
    "(" * 500 + "1" + ")" * 500,            # nesting DoS
    "x" * 10_000,                           # length DoS
    "1/0", "1e400", "sqrt(-1)",             # must raise, never return None
])
def test_rejects(payload):
    with pytest.raises(UnsafeExpression):
        evaluate(payload)

def test_returns_number_never_none():
    for expr in ["1+1", "rate*1000", "2^10"]:
        assert isinstance(evaluate(expr, {"rate": 0.0725}), float)
```

---

## Pitfalls to avoid

- **Treating `None` as "no result."** The single highest-risk pattern in this whole design. Raise.
- **Blacklisting keywords** (`exec`, `import`) instead of whitelisting node types — encoding tricks, Unicode lookalikes, and new syntax all defeat blacklists.
- **Regex validation as a substitute for parsing.** Use the AST.
- **Trusting client-side validation.** Re-validate server-side on every request.
- **Silently coercing with `float()`** in a loop — a `None` becomes an exception only if you're lucky enough to be near the boundary.
- **Float math for money.** Switch to `Decimal` and catch `InvalidOperation`.

The `security-hardening-prompt-builder` run returned a full OWASP-Top-10/NIST assessment scaffold for exactly this calculator-API scenario — useful as a review checklist to paste into a PR template or a code-review agent. Its most relevant lines are the `eval()`/`compile()`-on-user-input check, the whitelist-not-blacklist directive, and the A09 logging rule that would have caught every one of the silent `null`s above.

数学表达式与安全建议 · evaluated

回答

Here's a practical, OWASP-aligned approach to securing mathematical operations in a Python codebase, based on the security assessment generated and the validation behavior demonstrated by the tools.

## 1. Never use `eval()` / `exec()` on user input (OWASP A03: Injection)

The single biggest risk is allowing arbitrary code execution. This is exactly what a strict expression validator catches — the tools above show the contrast clearly:

| Input | Syntax validation result |
|---|---|
| `(2 + 3) * 4 / 2 - 1` | ✅ "Expression is syntactically valid." → evaluates to `9` |
| `__import__('os').system('rm -rf /')` | ❌ "Unexpected character `_`" — rejected before evaluation |

The key lesson: **validate the expression against a strict allowlist grammar *before* evaluating anything, and fail closed on any parse error.** The malicious input is blocked purely because the grammar only permits numbers, operators, and parentheses — names like `__import__` and `os` are not part of the grammar at all.

## 2. Recommended secure pattern

```python
import ast
import operator

# Allowlist: only safe operators, no names, attributes, or calls
_OPS = {
    ast.Add: operator.add, ast.Sub: operator.sub,
    ast.Mult: operator.mul, ast.Div: operator.truediv,
    ast.Pow: operator.pow, ast.Mod: operator.mod,
    ast.USub: operator.neg, ast.UAdd: operator.pos,
}
MAX_EXPR_LEN = 256
MAX_EXPONENT = 100          # prevent huge-exponent resource exhaustion

def validate(expr: str) -> ast.Expression:
    """Fail closed: raise on anything outside the allowlist grammar."""
    if not isinstance(expr, str) or len(expr) > MAX_EXPR_LEN:
        raise ValueError("invalid expression length")
    try:
        tree = ast.parse(expr, mode="eval")   # parse first, never eval raw
    except SyntaxError:
        raise ValueError("malformed expression")   # structured, no traceback leak
    return tree

def _eval(node):
    if isinstance(node, ast.Expression):
        return _eval(node.body)
    if isinstance(node, ast.Constant) and isinstance(node.value, (int, float)):
        return node.value
    if isinstance(node, ast.BinOp) and type(node.op) in _OPS:
        left, right = _eval(node.left), _eval(node.right)
        if isinstance(node.op, ast.Pow) and abs(right) > MAX_EXPONENT:
            raise ValueError("exponent too large")
        return _OPS[type(node.op)](left, right)
    if isinstance(node, ast.UnaryOp) and type(node.op) in _OPS:
        return _OPS[type(node.op)](_eval(node.operand))
    raise ValueError(f"disallowed construct: {type(node).__name__}")

def safe_eval(expr: str):
    return _eval(validate(expr))
```

This replaces raw `eval()` with an AST walk that **only** understands numeric constants and arithmetic operators. Names, function calls, attribute access, subscripts — everything that enables code execution — fall through to the `disallowed construct` error.

## 3. OWASP Top 10 mapping for this specific risk

- **A03 Injection** (primary): strict allowlist grammar + parse-before-eval + fail closed. ✅
- **A04 Insecure Design**: cap expression length and exponent size for defense-in-depth against resource exhaustion / "billion laughs"-style attacks.
- **A05 Security Misconfiguration**: return generic structured errors (e.g. `"malformed expression"`), never raw tracebacks or internals.
- **A09 Logging Failures**: log rejected expressions with the reason (invalid char, disallowed node) to detect probing/abuse — but never log sensitive data.

## 4. Verification workflow you can adopt

1. **Syntax gate first** — run every user expression through a validator (like the `validate_syntax` tool) and reject anything with errors *before* evaluation.
2. **Evaluate only whitelisted input** — confirm valid expressions produce expected results (e.g. `(2 + 3) * 4 / 2 - 1 → 9`).
3. **Negative test suite** — assert that payloads like `__import__('os')…`, `open('file')`, `().__class__`, and `9**9**9` all raise `ValueError` and never execute.
4. **Fuzz** with adversarial strings and `hypothesis` to prove the grammar holds.

## 5. Additional hardening

- Prefer battle-tested libraries for anything complex: `simpleeval`, `asteval`, or `numexpr` (with restricted scope) instead of hand-rolling once requirements grow.
- Run untrusted evaluation in a **subprocess with resource limits** (`resource.setrlimit` for CPU/memory) as isolation-in-depth.
- Keep dependencies patched (A06) — audit with `pip-audit` in CI.
- Add type/range validation (Pydantic) at the boundary where expressions enter your system.

## Caveat
These recommendations follow common OWASP/NIST best practices but should be customized to your environment and validated with penetration testing. Always treat the allowlist grammar as the source of truth — **if a construct isn't explicitly permitted, it must be rejected.**
